**Tool Call: Read file `backend/llm.py`**
Status: Completed

```edgeAI/backend/llm.py
     1	import os
     2	import httpx
     3	import json
     4	from dotenv import load_dotenv
     5	
     6	load_dotenv()
     7	
     8	OLLAMA_HOST = os.getenv("OLLAMA_HOST", "http://localhost:11434")
     9	OLLAMA_MODEL = os.getenv("OLLAMA_MODEL", "llama3.2:1b")
    10	
    11	def build_prompt(question: str, chunks: list[dict]) -> str:
    12	    context_blocks = []
    13	    for i, chunk in enumerate(chunks):
    14	        context_blocks.append(
    15	            f"[Source {i+1} — {chunk['source']}, page {chunk.get('page', '?')}]\n"
    16	            f"{chunk['text']}"
    17	        )
    18	    context = "\n\n".join(context_blocks)
    19	
    20	    prompt = f"""You are a precise document assistant. Answer questions using ONLY the context below.
    21	
    22	Rules:
    23	- Answer in one sentence or less — match the brevity of the question
    24	- If the answer is a number, date, name, or short phrase, give ONLY that value
    25	- If listing multiple items, separate them with commas or "and" — never use bullet points or newlines
    26	- No greetings, no preamble, no "According to...", no "Based on..."
    27	- Do not repeat the question
    28	- If the answer is not in the context, respond with exactly: Not found.
    29	
    30	Context:
    31	{context}
    32	
    33	Question: {question}
    34	Answer:"""
    35	
    36	    return prompt
    37	
    38	async def query_ollama_stream(question: str, chunks: list[dict]):
    39	    prompt = build_prompt(question, chunks)
    40	
    41	    payload = {
    42	        "model": OLLAMA_MODEL,
    43	        "prompt": prompt,
    44	        "stream": True,  #enable token streaming
    45	        "options": {
    46	            "temperature": 0.1,      # back to low — factual short answers need determinism
    47	            "top_p": 0.9,
    48	            "top_k": 40,
    49	            "repeat_penalty": 1.1,
    50	            "num_predict": 100,      # most answers are under 30 tokens, 100 is safe ceiling
    51	            "stop": ["\n\n", "Question:", "Context:"]  # stop generating at double newline or if it starts looping
    52	        }
    53	    }
    54	
    55	    async with httpx.AsyncClient(timeout=120.0) as client:
    56	        async with client.stream(
    57	            "POST",
    58	            f"{OLLAMA_HOST}/api/generate",
    59	            json=payload
    60	        ) as response:
    61	            async for line in response.aiter_lines():
    62	                if line.strip():
    63	                    try:
    64	                        data = json.loads(line)
    65	                        token = data.get("response", "")
    66	                        if token:
    67	                            yield token
    68	                        if data.get("done"):     # Ollama signals end of stream
    69	                            break
    70	                    except json.JSONDecodeError:
    71	                        continue
    72	
    73	async def query_ollama(question: str, chunks: list[dict]) -> str:
    74	    """
    75	        Sends the prompt to the Ollama LLM 
    76	    """
    77	    full_response = ""
    78	    async for token in query_ollama_stream(question, chunks):
    79	        full_response += token
    80	    return full_response.strip()
    81	
    82	
    83	

```


**Tool Call: Read file `backend/retriever.py`**
Status: Completed

```edgeAI/backend/retriever.py
     1	import os
     2	
     3	import faiss
     4	import numpy as np
     5	from dotenv import load_dotenv
     6	from sentence_transformers import CrossEncoder, SentenceTransformer
     7	
     8	load_dotenv()
     9	
    10	EMBED_MODEL = os.getenv("EMBED_MODEL")
    11	TOP_K = int(os.getenv("TOP_K", 10))
    12	RERANK_TOP_N = int(os.getenv("RERANK_TOP_N", 3))
    13	
    14	# Load models at module level to avoid reloading on every call
    15	embed_model = SentenceTransformer(EMBED_MODEL)
    16	
    17	reranker = CrossEncoder("cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2")
    18	
    19	def dense_search(    query: str, faiss_index, chunks: list[dict], top_k: int) -> list[tuple]:
    20	    query_vec = embed_model.encode(
    21	        [query],
    22	        normalize_embeddings=True,
    23	        convert_to_numpy=True
    24	    )
    25	    
    26	    import numpy as np
    27	    query_vec = np.array(query_vec, dtype=np.float32)
    28	
    29	    # FAISS returns distances and indices of top_k nearest neighbours
    30	    scores, indices = faiss_index.search(query_vec, top_k)
    31	
    32	    results = []
    33	    for idx, score in zip(indices[0], scores[0]):
    34	        if idx == -1:
    35	            continue  # no more results
    36	        results.append((chunks[idx], float(score)))
    37	
    38	    return results
    39	
    40	def sparse_search(    query: str, bm25_index, chunks: list[dict], top_k: int) -> list[tuple]:
    41	    tokenized_query = query.lower().split()
    42	    scores = bm25_index.get_scores(tokenized_query)
    43	
    44	    # indices of top_k highest scores
    45	    top_indices = np.argsort(scores)[::-1][:top_k]
    46	
    47	    results = []
    48	    for idx in top_indices:
    49	        if scores[idx] > 0:
    50	            results.append((chunks[idx], float(scores[idx])))
    51	
    52	    return results
    53	
    54	def hybrid_search(    query: str, faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks: list[dict]) -> list[tuple]:
    55	    dense_results = dense_search(query, faiss_index, chunks, TOP_K)
    56	    sparse_results = sparse_search(query, bm25_index, chunks, TOP_K)
    57	
    58	    # Build a map from chunk text → RRF score
    59	    rrf_scores = {}
    60	    chunk_map = {}  # text → chunk dict (to retrieve later)
    61	
    62	    # for dense
    63	    for rank, (chunk, score) in enumerate(dense_results):
    64	        key = chunk["text"]
    65	        rrf_scores[key] = rrf_scores.get(key, 0) + 1 / (rank + 60)
    66	        chunk_map[key] = chunk
    67	
    68	    # for sparse
    69	    for rank, (chunk, score) in enumerate(sparse_results):
    70	        key = chunk["text"]
    71	        rrf_scores[key] = rrf_scores.get(key, 0) + 1 / (rank + 60)
    72	        chunk_map[key] = chunk
    73	
    74	    # Sort by combined RRF score descending
    75	    sorted_keys = sorted(rrf_scores.keys(), key=lambda k: rrf_scores[k], reverse=True)
    76	    return [(chunk_map[k], rrf_scores[k]) for k in sorted_keys[:TOP_K]]
    77	
    78	
    79	def rerank(query: str, candidates: list[tuple]) -> list[tuple]:
    80	
    81	    if not candidates:
    82	        return []
    83	
    84	    # Cross-encoder needs (query, text) pairs
    85	    pairs = [(query, chunk["text"]) for chunk, _ in candidates]
    86	    scores = reranker.predict(pairs)
    87	
    88	    # Attach new scores and sort
    89	    reranked = sorted(
    90	        zip([c for c, _ in candidates], scores), key=lambda x: x[1], reverse=True
    91	    )
    92	    return reranked[:RERANK_TOP_N]
    93	
    94	
    95	def retrieve(query: str, faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks: list[dict]) -> list[dict]:
    96	
    97	    hyrid_results = hybrid_search(query, faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks)
    98	    reranked = rerank(query, hyrid_results)
    99	
   100	    if not reranked:
   101	        return [], 0.0
   102	
   103	    top_chunks = [chunk for chunk, _ in reranked]
   104	    confidence_score = float(reranked[0][1])  # highest relevance score from reranker
   105	
   106	    return top_chunks, confidence_score

```


## Assistant

<thinking>
I'm mapping out the architecture of this RAG system—FastAPI handling requests, PyMuPDF extracting text from PDFs, BM25 for keyword matching, sentence-transformers providing embeddings and a cross-encoder for reranking, FAISS indexing the vectors, and Ollama serving as the LLM backend via HTTP. The tricky part is that torch becomes an implicit dependency through sentence-transformers, which matters for Jetson Orin Nano compatibility.

The core issue is that Jetson Orin Nano runs ARM64 (aarch64) architecture with JetPack's custom CUDA version, but standard PyPI wheels are built for x86_64. While faiss-cpu has aarch64 wheels available on PyPI for recent versions, torch is the real blocker—NVIDIA doesn't publish standard aarch64 wheels for Jetson on PyPI, instead providing special JetPack-specific builds. Generic aarch64 torch wheels exist for ARM servers but they're CPU-only and won't leverage Jetson's Tegra GPU, which is the whole point of using the hardware.

sentence-transformers and its dependencies (transformers, numpy, scikit-learn, pillow) would need to be compatible with whatever torch installation you end up with.

For the other packages, faiss-cpu has aarch64 wheels available, pymupdf should install directly since it has manylinux_aarch64 wheels, rank-bm25 is pure Python so it works everywhere, and the FastAPI ecosystem (fastapi, uvicorn, python-multipart, httpx, python-dotenv) are all pure or near-pure Python packages that should work fine on Jetson as long as the Python version matches.

Since the project relies on Ollama for the LLM via HTTP rather than local torch inference, the GPU-heavy lifting is just the sentence-transformers embedding model and cross-encoder reranker, which are relatively small models that can run CPU-only on a Jetson Orin Nano if needed, though GPU acceleration would be faster. The user's web-based terminal access doesn't really affect the compatibility analysis—the main thing is verifying that all the requirements.txt versions work on Jetson's aarch64 architecture.

Newer Jetson Orin Nano boards typically come with JetPack 6.x (Ubuntu 22.04, Python 3.10, CUDA 12.2/12.6), while older models might have JetPack 5.1.x (Ubuntu 20.04, Python 3.8, CUDA 11.4). Since I can't directly access their board, I should guide them to check their JetPack version, Python version, and CUDA version via commands they can run in their web terminal, then provide compatibility guidance for both scenarios or help them adapt requirements.txt accordingly.

Now I'm checking PyPI for aarch64 wheel availability across the key packages—faiss-cpu, sentence-transformers, pymupdf, and the others. Most of these are pure Python or have widely available wheels, but I need to verify which ones have prebuilt aarch64 binaries to avoid compilation issues on the Jetson.
</thinking>

Let me check actual PyPI package availability for ARM64/aarch64 (which is the Jetson's CPU architecture) for the packages that matter most — these are compiled/binary packages, unlike the pure-Python ones.

    "description": "# Faiss\n\nFaiss is a library for efficient similarity search and clustering of dense vectors. It contains algorithms that search in sets of vectors of any size, up to ones that possibly do not fit in RAM. It also contains supporting code for evaluation and parameter tuning. Faiss is written in C++ with complete wrappers for Python/numpy. Some of the most useful algorithms are implemented on the GPU. It is developed primarily at Meta's [Fundamental AI Research](https://ai.facebook.com/) group.\n\n## News\n\nSee [CHANGELOG.md](CHANGELOG.md) for detailed information about latest features.\n\n## Introduction\n\nFaiss contains several methods for similarity search. It assumes that the instances are represented as vectors and are identified by an integer, and that the vectors can be compared with L2 (Euclidean) distances or dot products. Vectors that are similar to a query vector are those that have the lowest L2 distance or the highest dot product with the query vector. It also supports cosine similarity, since this is a dot product on normalized vectors.\n\nSome of the methods, like those based on binary vectors and compact quantization codes, solely use a compressed representation of the vectors and do not require to keep the original vectors. This generally comes at the cost of a less precise search but these methods can scale to billions of vectors in main memory on a single server. Other methods, like HNSW and NSG add an indexing structure on top of the raw vectors to make searching more efficient.\n\nThe GPU implementation can accept input from either CPU or GPU memory. On a server with GPUs, the GPU indexes can be used a drop-in replacement for the CPU indexes (e.g., replace `IndexFlatL2` with `GpuIndexFlatL2`) and copies to/from GPU memory are handled automatically. Results will be faster however if both input and output remain resident on the GPU. Both single and multi-GPU usage is supported.\n\n## Installing\n\nFaiss comes with precompiled libraries for Anaconda in Python, see [faiss-cpu](https://anaconda.org/pytorch/faiss-cpu), [faiss-gpu](https://anaconda.org/pytorch/faiss-gpu) and [faiss-gpu-cuvs](https://anaconda.org/pytorch/faiss-gpu-cuvs). The library is mostly implemented in C++, the only dependency is a [BLAS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Linear_Algebra_Subprograms) implementation. Optional GPU support is provided via CUDA or AMD ROCm, and the Python interface is also optional. The backend GPU implementations of NVIDIA [cuVS](https://github.com/rapidsai/cuvs) can also be enabled optionally. It compiles with cmake. See [INSTALL.md](INSTALL.md) for details.\n\n## How Faiss works\n\nFaiss is built around an index type that stores a set of vectors, and provides a function to search in them with L2 and/or dot product vector comparison. Some index types are simple baselines, such as exact search. Most of the available indexing structures correspond to various trade-offs with respect to\n\n- search time\n- search quality\n- memory used per index vector\n- training time\n- adding time\n- need for external data for unsupervised training\n\nThe optional GPU implementation provides what is likely (as of March 2017) the fastest exact and approximate (compressed-domain) nearest neighbor search implementation for high-dimensional vectors, fastest Lloyd's k-means, and fastest small k-selection algorithm known. [The implementation is detailed here](https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.08734).\n\n## Full documentation of Faiss\n\nThe following are entry points for documentation:\n\n- the full documentation can be found on the [wiki page](https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki), including a [tutorial](https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Getting-started), a [FAQ](https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/FAQ) and a [troubleshooting section](https://github.com/facebookresearch/faiss/wiki/Troubleshooting)\n- the [doxygen documentation](https://faiss.ai/) gives per-class information extracted from code comments\n- to reproduce results from our research papers, [Polysemous codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.01882) and [Billion-scale similarity search with GPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/1702.08734), refer to the [benchmarks README](benchs/README.md). For [\nLink and code: Fast indexing with graphs and compact regression codes](https://arxiv.org/abs/1804.09996), see the [link_and_code README](benchs/link_and_code)\n\n## Authors\n\nThe main authors of Faiss are:\n- [Hervé Jégou](https://github.com/jegou) initiated the Faiss project and wrote its first implementation\n- [Matthijs Douze](https://github.com/mdouze) implemented most of the CPU Faiss\n- [Jeff Johnson](https://github.com/wickedfoo) implemented all of the GPU Faiss\n- [Lucas Hosseini](https://github.com/beauby) implemented the binary indexes and the build system\n- [Chengqi Deng](https://github.com/KinglittleQ) implemented NSG, NNdescent and much of the additive quantization code.\n- [Alexandr Guzhva](https://github.com/alexanderguzhva) many optimizations: SIMD, memory allocation and layout, fast decoding kernels for vector codecs, etc.\n- [Gergely Szilvasy](https://github.com/algoriddle) build system, benchmarking framework.\n\n## Reference\n\nReferences to cite when you use Faiss in a research paper:\n```\n@article{douze2024faiss,\n      title={The Faiss library},\n      author={Matthijs Douze and Alexandr Guzhva and Chengqi Deng and Jeff Johnson and Gergely     "description": "![PyTorch Logo](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/docs/source/_static/img/pytorch-logo-dark.png)\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\nPyTorch is a Python package that provides two high-level features:\n- Tensor computation (like NumPy) with strong GPU acceleration\n- Deep neural networks built on a tape-based autograd system\n\nYou can reuse your favorite Python packages such as NumPy, SciPy, and Cython to extend PyTorch when needed.\n\nOur trunk health (Continuous Integration signals) can be found at [hud.pytorch.org](https://hud.pytorch.org/ci/pytorch/pytorch/main).\n\n<!-- toc -->\n\n- [More About PyTorch](#more-about-pytorch)\n  - [A GPU-Ready Tensor Library](#a-gpu-ready-tensor-library)\n  - [Dynamic Neural Networks: Tape-Based Autograd](#dynamic-neural-networks-tape-based-autograd)\n  - [Python First](#python-first)\n  - [Imperative Experiences](#imperative-experiences)\n  - [Fast and Lean](#fast-and-lean)\n  - [Extensions Without Pain](#extensions-without-pain)\n- [Installation](#installation)\n  - [Binaries](#binaries)\n    - [NVIDIA Jetson Platforms](#nvidia-jetson-platforms)\n  - [From Source](#from-source)\n    - [Prerequisites](#prerequisites)\n    - [Install Dependencies](#install-dependencies)\n    - [Get the PyTorch Source](#get-the-pytorch-source)\n    - [Install PyTorch](#install-pytorch)\n      - [Building the Documentation](#building-the-documentation)\n  - [Previous Versions](#previous-versions)\n- [Getting Started](#getting-started)\n- [Resources](#resources)\n- [Communication](#communication)\n- [Releases and Contributing](#releases-and-contributing)\n- [The Team](#the-team)\n- [License](#license)\n\n<!-- tocstop -->\n\n## More About PyTorch\n\n[Learn the basics of PyTorch](https://pytorch.org/tutorials/beginner/basics/intro.html)\n\nAt a granular level, PyTorch is a library that consists of the following components:\n\n| Component | Description |\n| ---- | --- |\n| [**torch**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/torch.html) | A Tensor library like NumPy, with strong GPU support |\n| [**torch.autograd**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/autograd.html) | A tape-based automatic differentiation library that supports all differentiable Tensor operations in torch |\n| [**torch.jit**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/jit.html) | A compilation stack (TorchScript) to create serializable and optimizable models from PyTorch code  |\n| [**torch.nn**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/nn.html) | A neural networks library deeply integrated with autograd designed for maximum flexibility |\n| [**torch.multiprocessing**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/multiprocessing.html) | Python multiprocessing, but with magical memory sharing of torch Tensors across processes. Useful for data loading and Hogwild training |\n| [**torch.utils**](https://pytorch.org/docs/stable/data.html) | DataLoader and other utility functions for convenience |\n\nUsually, PyTorch is used either as:\n\n- A replacement for NumPy to use the power of GPUs.\n- A deep learning research platform that provides maximum flexibility and speed.\n\nElaborating Further:\n\n### A GPU-Ready Tensor Library\n\nIf you use NumPy, then you have used Tensors (a.k.a. ndarray).\n\n![Tensor illustration](./docs/source/_static/img/tensor_illustration.png)\n\nPyTorch provides Tensors that can live either on the CPU or the GPU and accelerates the\ncomputation by a huge amount.\n\nWe provide a wide variety of tensor routines to accelerate and fit your scientific computation needs\nsuch as slicing, indexing, mathematical operations, linear algebra, reductions.\nAnd they are fast!\n\n### Dynamic Neural Networks: Tape-Based Autograd\n\nPyTorch has a unique way of building neural networks: using and replaying a tape recorder.\n\nMost frameworks such as TensorFlow, Theano, Caffe, and CNTK have a static view of the world.\nOne has to build a neural network and reuse the same structure again and again.\nChanging the way the network behaves means that one has to start from scratch.\n\nWith PyTorch, we use a technique called reverse-mode auto-differentiation, which allows you to\nchange the way your network behaves arbitrarily with zero lag or overhead. Our inspiration comes\nfrom several research papers on this topic, as well as current and past work such as\n[torch-autograd](https://github.com/twitter/torch-autograd),\n[autograd](https://github.com/HIPS/autograd),\n[Chainer](https://chainer.org), etc.\n\nWhile this technique is not unique to PyTorch, it's one of the fastest implementations of it to date.\nYou get the best of speed and flexibility for your crazy research.\n\n![Dynamic graph](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/docs/source/_static/img/dynamic_graph.gif)\n\n### Python First\n\nPyTorch is not a Python binding into a monolithic C++ framework.\nIt is built to be deeply integrated into Python.\nYou can use it naturally like you would use [NumPy](https://www.numpy.org/) / [SciPy](https://www.scipy.org/) / [scikit-learn](https://scikit-learn.org) etc.\nYou can write your new neural network layers in Python itself, using your favorite libraries\nand use packages such as [Cython](https://cython.org/) and [Numba](http://numba.pydata.org/).\nOur goal is to not reinvent the wheel where appropriate.\n\n### Imperative Experiences\n\nPyTorch is designed to be intuitive, linear in thought, and easy to use.\nWhen you execute a line of code, it gets executed. There isn't an asynchronous view of the world.\nWhen you drop into a debugger or receive error messages and stack traces, understanding them is straightforward.\nThe stack trace points to exactly where your code was defined.\nWe hope you never spend hours debugging your code because of bad stack traces or asynchronous and opaque execution engines.\n\n### Fast and Lean\n\nPyTorch has minimal framework overhead. We integrate acceleration libraries\nsuch as [Intel MKL](https://software.intel.com/mkl) and NVIDIA ([cuDNN](https://developer.nvidia.com/cudnn), [NCCL](https://developer.nvidia.com/nccl)) to maximize speed.\nAt the core, its CPU and GPU Tensor and neural network backends\nare mature and have been tested for years.\n\nHence, PyTorch is quite fast — whether you run small or large neural networks.\n\nThe memory usage in PyTorch is extremely efficient compared to Torch or some of the alternatives.\nWe've written custom memory allocators for the GPU to make sure that\nyour deep learning models are maximally memory efficient.\nThis enables you to train bigger deep learning models than before.\n\n### Extensions Without Pain\n\nWriting new neural network modules, or interfacing with PyTorch's Tensor API was designed to be straightforward\nand with minimal abstractions.\n\nYou can write new neural network layers in Python using the torch API\n[or your favorite NumPy-based libraries such as SciPy](https://pytorch.org/tutorials/advanced/numpy_extensions_tutorial.html).\n\nIf you want to write your layers in C/C++, we provide a convenient extension API that is efficient and with minimal boilerplate.\nNo wrapper code needs to be written. You can see [a tutorial here](https://pytorch.org/tutorials/advanced/cpp_extension.html) and [an example here](https://github.com/pytorch/extension-cpp).\n\n\n## Installation\n\n### Binaries\nCommands to install binaries via Conda or pip wheels are on our website: [https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/](https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/)\n\n\n#### NVIDIA Jetson Platforms\n\nPython wheels for NVIDIA's Jetson Nano, Jetson TX1/TX2, Jetson Xavier NX/AGX, and Jetson AGX Orin are provided [here](https://forums.developer.nvidia.com/t/pytorch-for-jetson-version-1-10-now-available/72048) and the L4T container is published [here](https://catalog.ngc.nvidia.com/orgs/nvidia/containers/l4t-pytorch)\n\nThey require JetPack 4.2 and above, and [@dusty-nv](https://github.com/dusty-nv) and [@ptrblck](https://github.com/ptrblck) are maintaining them.\n\n\n### From Source\n\n#### Prerequisites\nIf you are installing from source, you will need:\n- Python 3.8 or later (for Linux, Python 3.8.1+ is needed)\n- A compiler that fully supports C++17, such as clang or gcc (gcc 9.4.0 or newer is required)\n\nWe highly recommend installing an [Anaconda](https://www.anaconda.com/download) environment. You will get a high-quality BLAS library (MKL) and you get controlled dependency versions regardless of your Linux distro.\n\nIf you want to compile with CUDA support, [select a supported version of CUDA from our support matrix](https://pytorch.org/get-started/locally/), then install the following:\n- [NVIDIA CUDA](https://developer.nvidia.com/cuda-downloads)\n- [NVIDIA cuDNN](https://developer.nvidia.com/cudnn) v8.5 or above\n- [Compiler](https://gist.github.com/ax3l/9489132) compatible with CUDA\n\nNote: You could refer to the [cuDNN Support Matrix](https://docs.nvidia.com/deeplearning/cudnn/reference/support-matrix.html) for cuDNN versions with the various supported CUDA, CUDA driver and NVIDIA hardware\n\nIf you want to disable CUDA support, export the environment variable `USE_CUDA=0`.\nOther potentially useful environment variables may be found in `setup.py`.\n\nIf you are building for NVIDIA's Jetson platforms (Jetson Nano, TX1, TX2, AGX Xavier), Instructions to install PyTorch for Jetson Nano are [available here](https://devtalk.nvidia.com/default/topic/1049071/jetson-nano/pytorch-for-jetson-nano/)\n\nIf you want to compile with ROCm support, install\n- [AMD ROCm](https://rocm.docs.amd.com/en/latest/deploy/linux/quick_start.html) 4.0 and above installation\n- ROCm is currently supported only for Linux systems.\n\nIf you want to disable ROCm support, export the environment variable `USE_ROCM=0`.\nOther potentially useful environment variables may be found in `setup.py`.\n\n#### Install Dependencies\n\n**Common**\n\n```bash\nconda install cmake ninja\n# Run this command from the PyTorch directory after cloning the source code using the “Get the PyTorch Source“ section below\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\n\n**On Linux**\n\n```bash\nconda install intel::mkl-static intel::mkl-include\n# CUDA only: Add LAPACK support for the GPU if needed\nconda install -c pytorch magma-cuda110  # or the magma-cuda* that matches your CUDA version from https://anaconda.org/pytorch/repo\n\n# (optional) If using torch.compile with inductor/triton, install the matching version of triton\n# Run from the pytorch directory after cloning\nmake triton\n```\n\n**On MacOS**\n\n```bash\n# Add this package on intel x86 processor machines only\nconda install intel::mkl-static intel::mkl-include\n# Add these packages if torch.distributed is needed\nconda install pkg-config libuv\n```\n\n**On Windows**\n\n```bash\nconda install intel::mkl-static intel::mkl-include\n# Add these packages if torch.distributed is needed.\n# Distributed package support on Windows is a prototype feature and is subject to changes.\nconda install -c conda-forge libuv=1.39\n```\n\n#### Get the PyTorch Source\n```bash\ngit clone --recursive https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch\ncd pytorch\n# if you are updating an existing checkout\ngit submodule sync\ngit submodule update --init --recursive\n```\n\n#### Install PyTorch\n**On Linux**\n\nIf you would like to compile PyTorch with [new C++ ABI](https://gcc.gnu.org/onlinedocs/libstdc++/manual/using_dual_abi.html) enabled, then first run this command:\n```bash\nexport _GLIBCXX_USE_CXX11_ABI=1\n```\n\nIf you're compiling for AMD ROCm then first run this command:\n```bash\n# Only run this if you're compiling for ROCm\npython tools/amd_build/build_amd.py\n```\n\nInstall PyTorch\n```bash\nexport CMAKE_PREFIX_PATH=${CONDA_PREFIX:-\"$(dirname $(which conda))/../\"}\npython setup.py develop\n```\n\n> _Aside:_ If you are using [Anaconda](https://www.anaconda.com/distribution/#download-section), you may experience an error caused by the linker:\n>\n> ```plaintext\n> build/temp.linux-x86_64-3.7/torch/csrc/stub.o: file not recognized: file format not recognized\n> collect2: error: ld returned 1 exit status\n> error: command 'g++' failed with exit status 1\n> ```\n>\n> This is caused by `ld` from the Conda environment shadowing the system `ld`. You should use a newer version of Python that fixes this issue. The recommended Python version is 3.8.1+.\n\n**On macOS**\n\n```bash\npython3 setup.py develop\n```\n\n**On Windows**\n\nChoose Correct Visual Studio Version.\n\nPyTorch CI uses Visual C++ BuildTools, which come with Visual Studio Enterprise,\nProfessional, or Community Editions. You can also install the build tools from\nhttps://visualstudio.microsoft.com/visual-cpp-build-tools/. The build tools *do not*\ncome with Visual Studio Code by default.\n\nIf you want to build legacy python code, please refer to [Building on legacy code and CUDA](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/CONTRIBUTING.md#building-on-legacy-code-and-cuda)\n\n**CPU-only builds**\n\nIn this mode PyTorch computations will run on your CPU, not your GPU\n\n```cmd\nconda activate\npython setup.py develop\n```\n\nNote on OpenMP: The desired OpenMP implementation is Intel OpenMP (iomp). In order to link against iomp, you'll need to manually download the library and set up the building environment by tweaking `CMAKE_INCLUDE_PATH` and `LIB`. The instruction [here](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/docs/source/notes/windows.rst#building-from-source) is an example for setting up both MKL and Intel OpenMP. Without these configurations for CMake, Microsoft Visual C OpenMP runtime (vcomp) will be used.\n\n**CUDA based build**\n\nIn this mode PyTorch computations will leverage your GPU via CUDA for faster number crunching\n\n[NVTX](https://docs.nvidia.com/gameworks/content/gameworkslibrary/nvtx/nvidia_tools_extension_library_nvtx.htm) is needed to build Pytorch with CUDA.\nNVTX is a part of CUDA distributive, where it is called \"Nsight Compute\". To install it onto an already installed CUDA run CUDA installation once again and check the corresponding checkbox.\nMake sure that CUDA with Nsight Compute is installed after Visual Studio.\n\nCurrently, VS 2017 / 2019, and Ninja are supported as the generator of CMake. If `ninja.exe` is detected in `PATH`, then Ninja will be used as the default generator, otherwise, it will use VS 2017 / 2019.\n<br/> If Ninja is selected as the generator, the latest MSVC will get selected as the underlying toolchain.\n\nAdditional libraries such as\n[Magma](https://developer.nvidia.com/magma), [oneDNN, a.k.a. MKLDNN or DNNL](https://github.com/oneapi-src/oneDNN), and [Sccache](https://github.com/mozilla/sccache) are often needed. Please refer to the [installation-helper](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/tree/main/.ci/pytorch/win-test-helpers/installation-helpers) to install them.\n\nYou can refer to the [build_pytorch.bat](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/.ci/pytorch/win-test-helpers/build_pytorch.bat) script for some other environment variables configurations\n\n\n```cmd\ncmd\n\n:: Set the environment variables after you have downloaded and unzipped the mkl package,\n:: else CMake would throw an error as `Could NOT find OpenMP`.\nset CMAKE_INCLUDE_PATH={Your directory}\\mkl\\include\nset LIB={Your directory}\\mkl\\lib;%LIB%\n\n:: Read the content in the previous section carefully before you proceed.\n:: [Optional] If you want to override the underlying toolset used by Ninja and Visual Studio with CUDA, please run the following script block.\n:: \"Visual Studio 2019 Developer Command Prompt\" will be run automatically.\n:: Make sure you have CMake >= 3.12 before you do this when you use the Visual Studio generator.\nset CMAKE_GENERATOR_TOOLSET_VERSION=14.27\nset DISTUTILS_USE_SDK=1\nfor /f \"usebackq tokens=*\" %i in (`\"%ProgramFiles(x86)%\\Microsoft Visual Studio\\Installer\\vswhere.exe\" -version [15^,17^) -products * -latest -property installationPath`) do call \"%i\\VC\\Auxiliary\\Build\\vcvarsall.bat\" x64 -vcvars_ver=%CMAKE_GENERATOR_TOOLSET_VERSION%\n\n:: [Optional] If you want to override the CUDA host compiler\nset CUDAHOSTCXX=C:\\Program Files (x86)\\Microsoft Visual Studio\\2019\\Community\\VC\\Tools\\MSVC\\14.27.29110\\bin\\HostX64\\x64\\cl.exe\n\npython setup.py develop\n\n```\n\n##### Adjust Build Options (Optional)\n\nYou can adjust the configuration of cmake variables optionally (without building first), by doing\nthe following. For example, adjusting the pre-detected directories for CuDNN or BLAS can be done\nwith such a step.\n\nOn Linux\n```bash\nexport CMAKE_PREFIX_PATH=${CONDA_PREFIX:-\"$(dirname $(which conda))/../\"}\npython setup.py build --cmake-only\nccmake build  # or cmake-gui build\n```\n\nOn macOS\n```bash\nexport CMAKE_PREFIX_PATH=${CONDA_PREFIX:-\"$(dirname $(which conda))/../\"}\nMACOSX_DEPLOYMENT_TARGET=10.9 CC=clang CXX=clang++ python setup.py build --cmake-only\nccmake build  # or cmake-gui build\n```\n\n### Docker Image\n\n#### Using pre-built images\n\nYou can also pull a pre-built docker image from Docker Hub and run with docker v19.03+\n\n```bash\ndocker run --gpus all --rm -ti --ipc=host pytorch/pytorch:latest\n```\n\nPlease note that PyTorch uses shared memory to share data between processes, so if torch multiprocessing is used (e.g.\nfor multithreaded data loaders) the default shared memory segment size that container runs with is not enough, and you\nshould increase shared memory size either with `--ipc=host` or `--shm-size` command line options to `nvidia-docker run`.\n\n#### Building the image yourself\n\n**NOTE:** Must be built with a docker version > 18.06\n\nThe `Dockerfile` is supplied to build images with CUDA 11.1 support and cuDNN v8.\nYou can pass `PYTHON_VERSION=x.y` make variable to specify which Python version is to be used by Miniconda, or leave it\nunset to use the default.\n\n```bash\nmake -f docker.Makefile\n# images are tagged as docker.io/${your_docker_username}/pytorch\n```\n\nYou can also pass the `CMAKE_VARS=\"...\"` environment variable to specify additional CMake variables to be passed to CMake during the build.\nSee [setup.py](./setup.py) for the list of available variables.\n\n```bash\nCMAKE_VARS=\"BUILD_CAFFE2=ON BUILD_CAFFE2_OPS=ON\" make -f docker.Makefile\n```\n\n### Building the Documentation\n\nTo build documentation in various formats, you will need [Sphinx](http://www.sphinx-doc.org) and the\nreadthedocs theme.\n\n```bash\ncd docs/\npip install -r requirements.txt\n```\nYou can then build the documentation by running `make <format>` from the\n`docs/` folder. Run `make` to get a list of all available output formats.\n\nIf you get a katex error run `npm install katex`.  If it persists, try\n`npm install -g katex`\n\n> Note: if you installed `nodejs` with a different package manager (e.g.,\n`conda`) then `npm` will probably install a version of `katex` that is not\ncompatible with your version of `nodejs` and doc builds will fail.\nA combination of versions that is known to work is `node@6.13.1` and\n`katex@0.13.18`. To install the latter with `npm` you can run\n```npm install -g katex@0.13.18```\n\n### Previous Versions\n\nInstallation instructions and binaries for previous PyTorch versions may be found\non [our website](https://pytorch.org/previous-versions).\n\n\n## Getting Started\n\nThree-pointers to get you started:\n- [Tutorials: get you started with understanding and using PyTorch](https://pytorch.org/tutorials/)\n- [Examples: easy to understand PyTorch code across all domains](https://github.com/pytorch/examples)\n- [The API Reference](https://pytorch.org/docs/)\n- [Glossary](https://github.com/pytorch/pytorch/blob/main/GLOSSARY.md)\n\n## Resources\n\n* [PyTorch.org](https://pytorch.org/)\n* [PyTorch Tutorials](https://pytorch.org/tutorials/)\n* [PyTorch Examples](https://github.com/pytorch/examples)\n* [PyTorch Models](https://pytorch.org/hub/)\n* [Intro to Deep Learning with PyTorch from Udacity](https://www.udacity.com/course/deep-learning-pytorch--ud188)\n* [Intro to Machine Learning with PyTorch from Udacity](https://www.udacity.com/course/intro-to-machine-learning-nanodegree--nd229)\n* [Deep Neural Networks with PyTorch from Coursera](https://www.coursera.org/learn/deep-neural-networks-with-pytorch)\n* [PyTorch Twitter](https://twitter.com/PyTorch)\n* [PyTorch Blog](https://pytorch.org/blog/)\n* [PyTorch YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/channel/UCWXI5YeOsh03QvJ59PMaXFw)\n\n## Communication\n* Forums: Discuss implementations, research, etc. https://discuss.pytorch.org\n* GitHub Issues: Bug reports, feature requests, install issues, RFCs, thoughts, etc.\n* Slack: The [PyTorch Slack](https://pytorch.slack.com/) hosts a primary audience of moderate to experienced PyTorch users and developers for general chat, online discussions, collaboration, etc. If you are a beginner looking for help, the primary medium is [PyTorch Forums](https://discuss.pytorch.org). If you need a slack invite, please fill this form: https://goo.gl/forms/PP1AGvNHpSaJP8to1\n* Newsletter: No-noise, a one-way email newsletter with important announcements about PyTorch. The key insight is that while these generic aarch64 wheels should be glibc-compatible with JetPack 5 and 6, they'll run in CPU-only mode. For a RAG application using sentence-transformers with smaller models like MiniLM, this CPU-only approach should still be functional on an Orin Nano given its capable ARM processor, just slower than GPU acceleration would be—but it's a reasonable starting point.

If GPU acceleration becomes necessary, I'd need to grab the NVIDIA-specific Jetson torch wheels from their developer portal or forums rather than relying on PyPI, since the generic CUDA wheels for x86_64 won't work on aarch64 due to platform-specific dependencies. Now I'm checking Python version compatibility—sentence-transformers 3.0.1 likely supports Python 3.8+, but faiss-cpu 1.14.2 requires Python 3.10 or higher, which could be a constraint depending on what JetPack ships. Here's a full breakdown of your `backend/requirements.txt` compatibility with a Jetson Orin Nano.

## The core issue: it's not really about "compatible versions" — it's about **architecture + JetPack version**

Jetson Orin Nano runs on **ARM64 (aarch64)**, not x86_64. Most of your packages are version-pinned, but pip resolves wheels based on *(architecture, Python version, OS)*. So the real question for each package is: "Does this exact pinned version publish an `aarch64`/`arm64` wheel?" I checked PyPI directly for the packages that actually ship compiled binaries (the pure-Python ones are compatible everywhere).

## Package-by-package verdict

| Package | Compatible on Jetson (aarch64)? | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `fastapi==0.111.0` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python, universal wheel. |
| `uvicorn==0.29.0` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python core is fine everywhere. |
| `python-multipart==0.0.9` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python. |
| `pymupdf>=1.25.0` | ✅ Yes | Has `manylinux2014_aarch64` wheels. **Requires Python ≥3.9.** |
| `rank-bm25==0.2.2` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python, no compiled parts. |
| `httpx==0.27.0` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python. |
| `python-dotenv==1.0.1` | ✅ Yes | Pure Python. |
| `sentence-transformers==3.0.1` | ✅ Yes (itself) | Pure-Python wheel, but pulls in `torch`, `transformers`, `numpy`, `scikit-learn`, `scipy`, `Pillow` — see torch note below. |
| `faiss-cpu==1.14.2` | ⚠️ **Conditional** | Has an `aarch64` wheel, but **requires Python ≥3.10**. This is your tightest constraint. |
| `torch` (hidden dep, not pinned) | ⚠️ **Works, but CPU-only** | PyPI ships a generic `manylinux_aarch64` CPU wheel — it will install and run, but it will **not use the Jetson's GPU/CUDA cores**. See below. |

## The critical hidden dependency: `torch`

`torch` isn't in your `requirements.txt`, but `sentence-transformers` pulls it in transitively. This is the part most likely to bite you:

- **Generic PyPI `torch`** for aarch64 (e.g. `torch==2.3.1+manylinux2014_aarch64`) installs fine and *runs*, but it's a **CPU-only build**. It doesn't include Jetson's CUDA/Tegra-specific libraries, so your embedding + reranking calls in `retriever.py` will run on the ARM CPU cores only — not the Orin's GPU.
- For **GPU-accelerated** torch on Jetson, you need NVIDIA's Jetson-specific wheel, matched to your JetPack/CUDA version, e.g. for JetPack 6 (CUDA 12.6):
  ```
  pip3 install torch --index-url https://pypi.jetson-ai-lab.io/jp6/cu126
  ```
  (This index has had intermittent outages — if it's down, CPU-only fallback still works, just slower.)
- Given your models are small (`MiniLM`-class embedder + `cross-encoder/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2`), **CPU-only is actually a reasonable fallback** on an Orin Nano — it just won't be as fast as it could be.

## The other hidden dependency: Ollama itself

Your `llm.py` talks to Ollama over HTTP — Ollama is **not** a pip package, so it's outside `requirements.txt` entirely, but it's essential to the app running at all. Good news: Ollama has official native Jetson support (JetPack 5 and 6), installed via:
```
curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh
```
This auto-detects the Jetson and installs CUDA-accelerated inference. This is a separate install step you'll need to do directly on the board's terminal — it has nothing to do with your Python venv/requirements.txt.

## Why Python version matters here — check this first

The single most important thing to know before doing anything is **which JetPack version is flashed on your board**, because it fixes the default Python version:

| JetPack | Ubuntu | Default Python | `faiss-cpu==1.14.2` compatible? |
|---|---|---|---|
| JetPack 5.x | 20.04 | 3.8 | ❌ No — faiss-cpu 1.14.2 requires Python ≥3.10 |
| JetPack 6.x | 22.04 | 3.10 | ✅ Yes |

Run these in your web terminal to find out:
```bash
cat /etc/nv_tegra_release   # or: apt show nvidia-jetpack
python3 --version
uname -m                     # should print aarch64
```

If you're on **JetPack 5 / Python 3.8**, `pip install -r requirements.txt` will hard-fail on `faiss-cpu==1.14.2` (no matching wheel). You'd need to either upgrade to JetPack 6, install a separate Python 3.10+ via `deadsnakes`/pyenv/conda, or downgrade `faiss-cpu` to an older version that still supports 3.8 (e.g. `faiss-cpu==1.8.0`).

## Getting your code onto the board without ssh/scp

Since you only have a browser-based terminal:
- If your project is on GitHub/GitLab, just `git clone <url>` directly from the Jetson terminal — simplest option.
- For a single file like `requirements.txt`, you can paste it in directly with a heredoc:
  ```bash
  cat > requirements.txt << 'EOF'
  fastapi==0.111.0
  ...
  EOF
  ```
- If the web terminal is part of a Jupyter-like interface, there's often a file upload widget in the file browser pane — check for that.

## My recommendation

1. Confirm JetPack/Python version first (commands above).
2. If JetPack 6 (Python 3.10): your `requirements.txt` will install as-is via pip, just expect CPU-only torch unless you additionally install the Jetson-specific wheel.
3. Install Ollama separately via the official script (not pip).
4. If JetPack 5 (Python 3.8): you'll hit a wall on `faiss-cpu==1.14.2` — let me know and I can help adjust the pin to a compatible version, or you migrate to JetPack 6.

Want me to update `backend/requirements.txt` with a Jetson-friendly variant (e.g., unpinning/adjusting `faiss-cpu`, adding explicit `torch` with a comment pointing to the Jetson index)? I can do that once you confirm your JetPack version.

```edgeAI/backend/ingest.py
     1	import os
     2	import pickle
     3	import fitz 
     4	import faiss
     5	import numpy as np
     6	from rank_bm25 import BM25Okapi 
     7	from sentence_transformers import SentenceTransformer
     8	from dotenv import load_dotenv
     9	
    10	
    11	load_dotenv()
    12	
    13	EMBED_MODEL = os.getenv("EMBED_MODEL")
    14	INDEX_DIR   = os.getenv("INDEX_DIR")
    15	CHUNK_SIZE   = 150      #300
    16	CHUNK_OVERLAP = 20
    17	
    18	embed_model = SentenceTransformer(EMBED_MODEL)
    19	
    20	def extract_text_from_pdf(pdf_path: str) -> list[dict]:
    21	    doc = fitz.open(pdf_path)
    22	    pages = []
    23	    for i, page in enumerate(doc):
    24	        text = page.get_text("text").strip()
    25	        if text:
    26	            pages.append({"text": text, "page": i + 1})
    27	    doc.close()
    28	    return pages
    29	
    30	def chunk_text(pages: list[dict], source: str) -> list[dict]:
    31	    chunks = []
    32	    for page_data in pages:
    33	        words = page_data["text"].split()
    34	        start = 0
    35	        while start < len(words):
    36	            end = start + CHUNK_SIZE
    37	            chunk_words = words[start:end]
    38	            chunk_text = " ".join(chunk_words)
    39	
    40	            chunks.append({
    41	                "text": chunk_text,
    42	                "source": source,
    43	                "page": page_data["page"]
    44	            })
    45	
    46	            start += CHUNK_SIZE - CHUNK_OVERLAP
    47	    return chunks
    48	
    49	def build_index(chunks: list[dict]) -> tuple:
    50	    """
    51	        Takes chunks, embeds them, and builds two indexes:
    52	        1. FAISS index  — for dense semantic search
    53	        2. BM25 index   — for sparse keyword search
    54	    """
    55	    texts = [c["text"] for c in chunks]
    56	
    57	    ## Dense Index (FAISS)
    58	
    59	    print(f"Embedding {len(chunks)} chunks...")
    60	    embeddings = embed_model.encode(
    61	        texts,
    62	        show_progress_bar=True,
    63	        convert_to_numpy=True,
    64	        normalize_embeddings=True    # normalise for cosine similarity
    65	    )
    66	
    67	    dim = embeddings.shape[1]        # all-MiniLM gives 384-dim vectors
    68	    faiss_index = faiss.IndexFlatIP(dim)  # inner product for cosine similarity
    69	    faiss_index.add(embeddings)
    70	
    71	    ## Sparse Index (BM25)
    72	    tokenized = [text.lower().split() for text in texts]
    73	    bm25_index = BM25Okapi(tokenized)
    74	
    75	    return faiss_index, bm25_index, embeddings
    76	
    77	def save_index(faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks: list[dict], filename: str):
    78	    """ Save the FAISS index, BM25 index, and chunk metadata to disk(jetson) """
    79	    os.makedirs(INDEX_DIR, exist_ok=True)
    80	    
    81	    # Save FAISS index
    82	    # faiss.write_index(faiss_index, os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, f"{filename}_faiss.index"))
    83	    faiss.write_index(faiss_index, os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "faiss.index"))
    84	
    85	    # Save BM25 index and chunk metadata using pickle
    86	    with open(os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "bm25.pkl"), "wb") as f:
    87	        pickle.dump(bm25_index, f)
    88	
    89	    with open(os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "chunks.pkl"), "wb") as f:
    90	        pickle.dump(chunks, f)
    91	
    92	    ## save embeddings for potential reranking use
    93	    np.save(os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "embeddings.npy"), 
    94	            embed_model.encode([c["text"] for c in chunks],
    95	                                convert_to_numpy=True,
    96	                                normalize_embeddings=True))
    97	    
    98	    print(f"Index saved for {filename} with {len(chunks)} chunks.")
    99	
   100	def load_index():
   101	    """load the persistent indexes and metadata from disk"""
   102	    faiss_path = os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "faiss.index")
   103	    bm25_path = os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "bm25.pkl")
   104	    chunks_path = os.path.join(INDEX_DIR, "chunks.pkl")
   105	
   106	    if not all(os.path.exists(p) for p in [faiss_path, bm25_path, chunks_path]):
   107	        return None, None, None
   108	    
   109	    faiss_index = faiss.read_index(faiss_path)
   110	
   111	    with open(bm25_path, "rb") as f:
   112	        bm25_index = pickle.load(f)
   113	
   114	    with open(chunks_path, "rb") as f:
   115	        chunks = pickle.load(f)
   116	
   117	    print(f"Loaded index with {len(chunks)} chunks.")
   118	    return faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks
   119	
   120	def ingest_pdf(pdf_path: str, filename: str) -> int:
   121	    """ Main function to ingest a PDF and build indexes """
   122	
   123	    pages = extract_text_from_pdf(pdf_path)
   124	    chunks = chunk_text(pages, source=filename)
   125	    faiss_index, bm25_index, embeddings = build_index(chunks)
   126	    save_index(faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks, filename)
   127	    return len(chunks)
   128	

```

```edgeAI/backend/main.py
     1	import os
     2	import shutil
     3	from fastapi import FastAPI, UploadFile, File, HTTPException
     4	from fastapi.middleware.cors import CORSMiddleware
     5	from fastapi.responses import FileResponse, StreamingResponse
     6	from fastapi.staticfiles import StaticFiles
     7	from dotenv import load_dotenv
     8	
     9	from models import QueryRequest, QueryResponse, IngestResponse, Citation
    10	from ingest import ingest_pdf, load_index
    11	from retriever import retrieve
    12	from confidence import score_confidence, is_confident
    13	from llm import query_ollama_stream
    14	
    15	load_dotenv()
    16	
    17	UPLOAD_DIR = os.getenv("UPLOAD_DIR", "../data/uploads")
    18	os.makedirs(UPLOAD_DIR, exist_ok=True)
    19	
    20	app = FastAPI(title = "Campus Handbook Bot")
    21	
    22	app.add_middleware(
    23	    CORSMiddleware,
    24	    allow_origins = ["http://localhost:5173", "http://localhost:3000"],
    25	    allow_methods = ["*"],
    26	    allow_headers = ["*"],
    27	)
    28	
    29	# Load index into memory at startup (if it exists)
    30	faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks = load_index()
    31	
    32	## API ENDPOINTS
    33	
    34	@app.post("/ingest", response_model=IngestResponse)
    35	async def ingest(file: UploadFile = File(...)):
    36	    global faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks        # ← this line is critical
    37	
    38	    if not file.filename.endswith(".pdf"):
    39	        raise HTTPException(400, "Only PDF files are accepted")
    40	
    41	    save_path = os.path.join(UPLOAD_DIR, file.filename)
    42	    with open(save_path, "wb") as f:
    43	        shutil.copyfileobj(file.file, f)
    44	
    45	    num_chunks = ingest_pdf(save_path, file.filename)
    46	
    47	    faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks = load_index()  # reload into globals
    48	
    49	    return IngestResponse(
    50	        message="Ingestion complete",
    51	        chunks_indexed=num_chunks,
    52	        filename=file.filename
    53	    )
    54	
    55	@app.post("/query")
    56	async def query(request: QueryRequest):
    57	    """
    58	    Accepts a question, retrieves relevant chunks,
    59	    checks confidence, then streams the LLM answer.
    60	    """
    61	    global faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks 
    62	    if faiss_index is None:
    63	        raise HTTPException(400, "No documents indexed yet. Please upload a PDF first.")
    64	
    65	    #retrieve + rerank
    66	    top_chunks, confidence = retrieve(
    67	        request.question, faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks
    68	    )
    69	
    70	    # Confidence gate: if retrieval is weak, don't call the LLM
    71	    if not is_confident(confidence):
    72	        async def not_found_stream():
    73	            yield "I couldn't find this in the handbook."
    74	        return StreamingResponse(not_found_stream(), media_type="text/plain")
    75	
    76	    # Stream LLM response token by token
    77	    return StreamingResponse(
    78	        query_ollama_stream(request.question, top_chunks),
    79	        media_type="text/plain"
    80	    )
    81	
    82	@app.get("/citations")
    83	async def get_citations(question: str):
    84	    """
    85	    Separate endpoint to get citations for a question.
    86	    Frontend calls this alongside /query to display sources.
    87	    """
    88	    global faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks 
    89	    if faiss_index is None:
    90	        return {"citations": [], "confidence": 0.0, "found": False}
    91	
    92	    top_chunks, confidence = retrieve(
    93	        question, faiss_index, bm25_index, chunks
    94	    )
    95	
    96	    found = is_confident(confidence)
    97	    citations = [
    98	        Citation(
    99	            text = c["text"][:300] + "..." if len(c["text"]) > 300 else c["text"],
   100	            source = c["source"],
   101	            page = c.get("page")
   102	        )   
   103	        for c in top_chunks
   104	    ]   if found else []
   105	
   106	    return {
   107	        "citations": [c.dict() for c in citations],
   108	        "confidence": round(confidence, 3),
   109	        "found": found
   110	    }
   111	
   112	@app.get("/health")
   113	async def health():
   114	    return {
   115	        "status": "ok",
   116	        "index_loaded": faiss_index is not None,
   117	        "chunk_count": len(chunks) if chunks else 0
   118	    }
   119	
   120	# Serve built frontend from public/dist
   121	FRONTEND_DIR = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), "public", "dist")
   122	
   123	if os.path.isdir(FRONTEND_DIR):
   124	    app.mount("/assets", StaticFiles(directory=os.path.join(FRONTEND_DIR, "assets")), name="assets")
   125	
   126	    @app.get("/{full_path:path}")
   127	    async def serve_frontend(full_path: str):
   128	        file_path = os.path.join(FRONTEND_DIR, full_path.lstrip("/"))
   129	        if os.path.isfile(file_path):
   130	            return FileResponse(file_path)
   131	        return FileResponse(os.path.join(FRONTEND_DIR, "index.html"))
```

     1	import os

     3	
     4	load_dotenv()
     5	
     6	THRESHOLD = float(os.getenv("CONFIDENCE_THRESHOLD", 0.35))
     7	
     8	def score_confidence(similarity_score: list[float]) -> float:
     9	    if not similarity_score:
    10	        return 0.0
    11	    return float(max(similarity_score))
    12	
    13	def is_confident(confidence: float) -> bool:
    14	    return confidence >= THRESHOLD

## Assistant

<thinking>
Now I'm going through the deploy script step by step to identify any potential issues by comparing it against the actual codebase. The script installs zstd, pulls down and runs the Ollama installer, then starts the Ollama service in the background with a brief wait period before proceeding.

Next, it pulls the llama3.2:1b model, sets up a Python virtual environment with system packages enabled, and installs the required dependencies including sentence-transformers and transformers with specific version pinning, plus the backend requirements. Then it rebuilds the FAISS index by loading the pickled chunks, generating embeddings using the embed model, creating a flat IP index, and writing it to disk. Finally, it starts the backend server using uvicorn on localhost.

I'm noticing a critical issue in Step 6 — the pip install command is missing `==` operators between the package names and versions, so `sentence-transformers2.7.0` and `transformers4.39.3` would be treated as invalid package names that don't exist on PyPI. This needs to be fixed to use proper version specifiers. Also, there's a question about why sentence-transformers is being pinned to 2.7.0 here when the requirements.txt might already specify a different version like 3.0.1. specified compatible versions. The `--system-site-packages` flag is a deliberate choice for Jetson setups where CUDA-optimized packages like torch are pre-installed at the system level, and the venv reuses those rather than rebuilding them from source. icit `pip install sentence-transformers==2.7.0` might trigger a torch reinstall if version constraints differ, though typically both versions require `torch>=1.11.0` so it should be safe. The real concern is whether the Jetson board already has a system-wide GPU-enabled torch installed—if not, `--system-site-packages` won't help and pip will pull a generic CPU-only wheel from PyPI when resolving dependencies.

There's also a Python indentation error in Step 7 where the code inside the `with` block isn't properly indented, causing a syntax error.

Beyond that, Step 7 is problematic because rebuilding the FAISS index triggers a model download from Hugging Face Hub at import time, which requires internet access on the Jetson device — something that might not be available on first deployment with a fresh environment. The reranker model loads separately when the backend starts via uvicorn, since `main.py` imports `retriever.py` which instantiates the CrossEncoder. This also requires internet access on first run, and if the Jetson lacks persistent connectivity or the download takes too long, the uvicorn startup could hang before the health check completes.

## Bugs found

**1. Fatal typo — Step 6 pip install will error immediately**
```bash
pip install --no-user sentence-transformers2.7.0 transformers4.39.3
```
Missing `==`. Pip will look for packages literally named `sentence-transformers2.7.0` and `transformers4.39.3` on PyPI, find nothing, and exit non-zero. Since the script has `set -e`, **the whole script dies right here, every time.**

**2. Silent bug — your version fix gets undone one line later**
Even after fixing the typo to `sentence-transformers==2.7.0 transformers==4.39.3`, the very next line:
```bash
pip install --no-user -r backend/requirements.txt
```
installs `backend/requirements.txt`, which still pins `sentence-transformers==3.0.1`. Pip will **upgrade back to 3.0.1**, silently discarding the exact fix you spent attempts finding. This is the most dangerous bug because it doesn't error — it just quietly reintroduces the problem you already solved.

**3. Fatal — Python `IndentationError` in Step 7**
```python
with open(f'{INDEX_DIR}/chunks.pkl', 'rb') as f:
chunks = pickle.load(f)
```
`chunks = pickle.load(f)` is not indented under the `with`. Passing multi-line Python through `python3 -c "..."` like this will raise `IndentationError` and abort the index rebuild.

**4. Missing step — `.env` is never placed on the board**
Your `.gitignore` excludes `.env`, `backend/.env`, and everything under `data/index/*` except `.gitkeep`. If you're getting code onto the Jetson via `git clone` (or anything that respects `.gitignore`), then **`backend/.env` and your prebuilt `chunks.pkl`/`bm25.pkl`/`faiss.index` won't exist on the board at all.** The script never creates `.env` or checks for these files — it'll fail deep into Step 7 with `FileNotFoundError`/`SentenceTransformer(None)` errors, well after you've already spent time on `apt-get`, Ollama install, and model pulls.

**5. Timing risk — `sleep 15` before the health check is likely too short**
`main.py` imports `retriever.py` at module load time, which loads **two transformer models** (`all-MiniLM-L6-v2` embedder + `ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2` cross-encoder) *before* Uvicorn starts accepting connections. On a Jetson with CPU-only torch (see earlier discussion) and a possible cold Hugging Face Hub download, this can easily take longer than 15s. With `set -e`, a failed `curl` here kills the script and looks like a deployment failure when it's really just "not warmed up yet."

**6. Unverified assumption — `--system-site-packages`**
This only helps if the Jetson already has a JetPack/CUDA-enabled `torch` installed at the system level. If it doesn't, the flag does nothing useful and just risks pulling in a mismatched system `numpy`. Worth verifying before relying on it.

**7. No network/version preflight** — no check that `huggingface.co` and `ollama.com` are reachable, and no check that `python3` is 3.10+ (required by `faiss-cpu==1.14.2`, per our earlier check). If either is wrong, you find out 5+ minutes into the run instead of in the first 10 seconds.

Let me fix `requirements.txt` and write a hardened deploy script + a fast preflight script.

```
#!/bin/bash
# Run this FIRST, before deploy_jetson.sh, to fail fast (seconds, not hours)
# if something fundamental is wrong. Run from the project root (edgeAI/).
set -u
FAIL=0

check() {
    local desc="$1"; local cmd="$2"
    printf "%-55s" "$desc"
    if eval "$cmd" >/tmp/preflight_out 2>&1; then
        echo "OK"
    else
        echo "FAIL"
        sed 's/^/    /' /tmp/preflight_out
        FAIL=1
    fi
}

echo "== Architecture / Python =="
check "Architecture is aarch64"          "[ \"\$(uname -m)\" = aarch64 ]"
check "python3 is >= 3.10 (faiss-cpu needs it)" \
    "python3 -c 'import sys; assert sys.version_info >= (3,10), sys.version'"

echo
echo "== Network reachability =="
check "Can reach ollama.com"             "curl -fsS -m 8 -o /dev/null https://ollama.com"
check "Can reach huggingface.co"         "curl -fsS -m 8 -o /dev/null https://huggingface.co"
check "Can reach pypi.org"               "curl -fsS -m 8 -o /dev/null https://pypi.org"

echo
echo "== Required project files present =="
check "backend/.env exists"              "[ -f backend/.env ]"
check "backend/requirements.txt exists"  "[ -f backend/requirements.txt ]"
check "data/index/chunks.pkl exists"     "[ -f data/index/chunks.pkl ]"
check "data/index/bm25.pkl exists"       "[ -f data/index/bm25.pkl ]"
check "data/index/faiss.index exists"    "[ -f data/index/faiss.index ]"

echo
echo "== Disk / memory headroom =="
check "At least 5GB free disk"           "[ \$(df --output=avail -k . | tail -1) -gt 5000000 ]"

echo
if [ "$FAIL" -eq 1 ]; then
    echo "❌ Preflight FAILED — fix the items above before running deploy_jetson.sh"
    echo "   (This took seconds instead of burning your test window.)"
    exit 1
else
    echo "✅ Preflight passed — safe to run deploy_jetson.sh"
fi

```


**Tool Call: deploy\_jetson.sh**
Status: Completed

Diff: edgeAI/deploy_jetson.sh
```
#!/bin/bash
# Run from the project root (edgeAI/). Run preflight_check.sh first.
set -e

# --- Configurable persistent cache paths -----------------------------------
# If your web-terminal session/container resets between test windows but the
# filesystem under the project persists, point these at a project-local dir
# so you don't re-download the embedder/reranker/Ollama model every attempt.
export HF_HOME="${HF_HOME:-$PWD/.cache/huggingface}"
export OLLAMA_MODELS="${OLLAMA_MODELS:-$PWD/.cache/ollama}"
mkdir -p "$HF_HOME" "$OLLAMA_MODELS"

wait_for() {
    # wait_for <url> <label> <max_seconds>
    local url="$1" label="$2" max="$3" waited=0
    until curl -sf "$url" -o /dev/null 2>/dev/null; do
        waited=$((waited + 2))
        if [ "$waited" -ge "$max" ]; then
            echo "❌ Timed out waiting for $label ($url) after ${max}s"
            return 1
        fi
        sleep 2
    done
    echo "✅ $label is up after ${waited}s"
}

echo "= Step 0: Sanity checks =="
[ -f backend/.env ] || { echo "❌ backend/.env missing — copy it before continuing (see chat for contents)."; exit 1; }
[ -f data/index/chunks.pkl ] || { echo "❌ data/index/chunks.pkl missing — copy your prebuilt index files (they're gitignored) before continuing."; exit 1; }
PYVER=$(python3 -c 'import sys; print(sys.version_info[:2]>=(3,10))')
[ "$PYVER" = "True" ] || { echo "❌ python3 is older than 3.10 — faiss-cpu==1.14.2 will not install."; exit 1; }

echo "= Step 1: Install zstd =="
apt-get update && apt-get install -y zstd

echo "= Step 2: Install Ollama =="
curl -fsSL https://ollama.com/install.sh | sh

echo "= Step 3: Start Ollama =="
ollama serve > /tmp/ollama.log 2>&1 &
wait_for "http://localhost:11434" "Ollama server" 30

echo "= Step 4: Pull model =="
ollama pull llama3.2:1b

echo "= Step 5: Setup Python =="
python3 -m venv .venv --system-site-packages
source .venv/bin/activate

echo "= Step 6: Install packages (single source of truth: requirements.txt) =="
pip install -r backend/requirements.txt

echo "= Step 7: Rebuild FAISS index =="
cd backend
python3 - <<'PYEOF'
import pickle
import faiss
import numpy as np
from ingest import embed_model

INDEX_DIR = "../data/index"

with open(f"{INDEX_DIR}/chunks.pkl", "rb") as f:
    chunks = pickle.load(f)

texts = [c["text"] for c in chunks]
embeddings = embed_model.encode(
    texts, convert_to_numpy=True, normalize_embeddings=True
)
embeddings = np.ascontiguousarray(embeddings, dtype=np.float32)

index = faiss.IndexFlatIP(embeddings.shape[1])
index.add(embeddings)
faiss.write_index(index, f"{INDEX_DIR}/faiss.index")
print(f"Done: {index.ntotal} vectors")
PYEOF

echo "= Step 8: Start backend =="
python3 -m uvicorn main:app --host 0.0.0.0 --port 8000 > /tmp/uvicorn.log 2>&1 &
if wait_for "http://localhost:8000/health" "Backend" 90; then
    curl http://localhost:8000/health
else
    echo "---- last 50 lines of uvicorn log ----"
    tail -n 50 /tmp/uvicorn.log
    exit 1
fi

```

outside any venv. If that fails or reports `cuda.is_available() == False`, your embedder/reranker will run CPU-only regardless of this flag — which is fine functionally, just slower. If you want GPU acceleration, that's the point where you'd install the Jetson-specific torch wheel (from `pypi.jetson-ai-lab.io`) as discussed earlier, before Step 6.

**Suggested workflow for your next window:** run `preflight_check.sh` first (seconds). If it passes, run `deploy_jetson.sh`. If it fails, you'll know exactly which precondition is missing without having spent any of your 3 hours on Ollama installs or model downloads.

I haven't run these on real Jetson hardware (I don't have access to your board), so I can't guarantee a clean pass — but every concrete bug I could find by static review is now fixed. Let me know how the run goes and I can help debug the log output if something still trips.

    58	The key things to understand before you start building:
    59	
    60	Unified memory is the big deal. Unlike a desktop where CPU has its own RAM and GPU has VRAM, Jetson uses a single memory pool shared between both. This means a 8GB Orin Nano actually gives your LLM the full 8GB — no separate VRAM limit. That's why you can run a 7B model at 4-bit quantization on it at all.
    61	
    62	JetPack is your OS. When they hand you a Jetson, it'll be running JetPack — which is Ubuntu 22.04 with NVIDIA's CUDA, cuDNN, TensorRT, and a bunch of other libraries pre-installed. Don't install a vanilla Ubuntu on it or you'll lose all the GPU tooling. Think of it as their equivalent of a driver bundle.
    63	
    64	jetson-containers is your best friend. It's an NVIDIA-maintained GitHub repo of pre-built Docker containers specifically for Jetson — Ollama, LlamaIndex, ChromaDB, Whisper, all pre-compiled for the ARM64 + CUDA combo. This saves you hours because you can't just pip install most ML libraries on Jetson's ARM architecture — they need to be compiled against Jetson's CUDA libraries specifically.
    65	
    66	TOPS is marketing, memory is the real constraint. For your RAG project, what limits you isn't raw compute — it's how big a model you can fit in memory. On 8GB, you're looking at 7B parameter models at 4-bit quantization (roughly 4–5GB), leaving 3GB for your embedding model + ChromaDB + the app itself. Tight but doable.

   106	what is ollama, what it does?
   107	
   108	Assistant:
   109	
   110	Ollama is basically a tool that lets you run LLMs locally on your machine with minimal setup — think of it as the "Docker for LLMs."
   111	
   112	What it actually does:
   113	
   114	Without Ollama, running a local LLM means manually downloading model weights, figuring out the right quantization format, writing inference code, managing GPU memory, setting up an API server yourself — it's a pain. Ollama packages all of that into one simple tool.
   115	
   116	You just do:
   117	
   118	ollama run mistral
   119	
   120	And it downloads the model, loads it into memory, and gives you a chat interface. That's it.
   121	
   122	Under the hood it:
   123	
   124	Downloads models in GGUF format (a compressed, quantized format optimized for CPU/GPU inference)
   125	
   126	Uses llama.cpp as the inference engine underneath
   127	
   128	Exposes a local REST API at localhost:11434 that mimics OpenAI's API format
   129	
   130	Manages loading/unloading models from memory automatically
   131	
   132	The API part is what matters for your project. Once Ollama is running, you call it like:
   133	
   134	import requests
   135	response = requests.post("http://localhost:11434/api/generate", json={
   136	    "model": "mistral",
   137	    "prompt": "your prompt here"
   138	})
   139	
   140	Or via the ollama Python library. LangChain and LlamaIndex both have native Ollama integrations, so plugging it into a RAG pipeline is straightforward.
   141	
   142	On Jetson specifically — Ollama has ARM64 + CUDA support, so it runs on Jetson and actually uses the GPU. The jetson-containers repo has a pre-built container for it so you don't have to compile anything yourself.
   143	
   144	Essentially in your RAG stack: Ollama handles the LLM piece, you handle the retrieval piece (chunking docs, embedding, vector search), and you wire them together. It's the easiest way to get a local LLM up and running without writing any inference code.

   148	i was thinking to build this rag system for exam copy evaluation, a system where the pdf of correct or acceptable answers is ingested and based on that the scanned student exam copy pdf is evaluated and then correctness and performance is measured. my doubt is that is it possibl?e to parse texts form a scanned page pdf?
   149	
   150	Assistant:
   151	
   152	Yes it's possible, and you've actually landed on a genuinely useful project idea. Let me break down the specific challenge you're asking about.
   153	
   154	The scanned PDF problem
   155	
   156	A scanned PDF is just an image embedded in a PDF container — there's no actual text layer in it. So yes, you need OCR (Optical Character Recognition) to extract text from it before anything else. This is a solved problem, the question is just which tool and how well it handles handwritten exam copies.
   157	
   158	Here's the thing though — there are two very different cases:
   159	
   160	Case 1: Typed/printed student answers — OCR works excellently. Tesseract (open source, runs on Jetson) or EasyOCR will give you 95%+ accuracy on clean scans.
   161	
   162	Case 2: Handwritten student answers — this is significantly harder. Standard OCR tools like Tesseract are trained on printed text and struggle badly with handwriting, especially messy student handwriting. This is where your project either gets interesting or hits a wall.
   163	
   164	For handwritten copies your options are:
   165	
   166	TrOCR (Microsoft's transformer-based OCR) — specifically trained on handwritten text, runs locally, much better than Tesseract on handwriting
   167	
   168	PaddleOCR — has a handwriting mode, good accuracy, runs on ARM/CUDA
   169	
   170	A VLM (Vision Language Model) like moondream2 or LLaVA — instead of OCR then text processing, you feed the page image directly to a vision model and ask it to read and evaluate. This actually skips the OCR step entirely and is arguably the smarter architecture for this problem
   171	
   172	The smarter architecture honestly
   173	
   174	Instead of:
   175	
   176	Scanned PDF → OCR → text → RAG pipeline → evaluation
   177	
   178	Consider:
   179	
   180	Scanned PDF → page images → VLM → evaluation against answer key
   181	
   182	A small VLM on Jetson (moondream2 is only 1.8B parameters) can look at a handwritten page image and understand its content well enough to compare against a text answer key. This sidesteps the OCR quality problem entirely and is a more novel approach — nobody at the internship level is doing this.
   183	
   184	What makes your idea strong
   185	
   186	The exam evaluation angle is a real problem in India specifically — schools and universities deal with thousands of handwritten copies. An on-device system means a school with no cloud access or budget for APIs could use it. That's a compelling pitch.
   187	
   188	One thing to nail down early — are the exam copies handwritten or typed? That changes the architecture quite a bit.

   194	Assistant:
   195	
   196	You're right to push back on that — keeping the RAG pipeline is the correct call, both technically and for the internship. Let me rethink this properly.
   197	
   198	The full architecture with both:
   199	
   200	Answer key PDF (typed)
   201	       ↓
   202	   Chunk + embed → Vector DB (ChromaDB/FAISS)
   203	                         ↑

   209	                         ↓

   211	                         ↓
   212	              Evaluation: score + specific mistakes + citations
   213	
   214	So the RAG pipeline's job here is very specific and actually quite elegant — when the LLM evaluates a student's answer, it's not evaluating from memory or hallucinating what a correct answer looks like. It's retrieving the actual relevant chunks from the answer key and grounding its judgment in that. That's exactly what RAG is for.
   215	
   216	Why this is better than just prompting the LLM directly:
   217	
   218	Without RAG, you'd dump the entire answer key into the prompt context. On a constrained model like Mistral 7B on Jetson, that context window fills up fast and the model starts losing track of earlier content. With RAG, you only pull the 3-4 most relevant chunks from the answer key for each question being evaluated — keeps the context tight and the evaluation focused.
   219	
   220	The citation angle is actually your killer feature
   221	
   222	When the system says "your answer about photosynthesis is incomplete," it can also say "according to page 3, section 2 of the answer key, the correct explanation includes the role of chlorophyll." That's a cited evaluation, which is exactly what the project brief says — "Document Q&A with citations, entirely on-device." You're literally fulfilling the brief.
   223	
   224	Where OCR and VLM both fit in this architecture
   225	
   226	You don't have to choose between them — use both at different stages:
   227	
   228	OCR (PaddleOCR or TrOCR) for extracting text from the student copy to query the vector DB
   229	
   230	Optionally a VLM pass for pages where OCR confidence is low (messy handwriting sections)
   231	
   232	The OCR output becomes the query, the vector DB retrieves relevant answer key chunks, and the LLM does the final evaluation with those chunks as context.
   233

   299

   301

If PaddleOCR misreads a student's handwriting, the RAG query is garbage, the retrieval is wrong, and the evaluation is wrong — and the 1B model won't be smart enough to recover from noisy input the way a 7B would. Mitigation: scope v1 to printed/typed answer sheets only, not handwritten. Be explicit about this constraint in your problem statement.
   303	
   304	Jetson deployment is a separate non-trivial task. People underestimate this. Getting it running on your laptop is one thing — cross-compiling, setting up JetPack, managing ARM64 Docker containers, and making sure Ollama actually uses the GPU on Jetson is easily a week of work on its own. Mitigation: allocate dedicated time in week 5-6 specifically for this, don't leave it for the end.

   331	
   332	First, the honest reality of Llama 3.2 1B
   333	
   334	1B is not a reasoning model — it's a completion model that follows simple instructions. For RAG it actually works if you keep prompts tight, because the model's job is just: "here are 3 chunks of text, answer this question using only these chunks, cite which chunk you used." That's a simple enough task for 1B. Where it'll fail is complex multi-step reasoning, long contexts, and structured JSON output. Design around these weaknesses from day one.
   335	
   336	Use case recommendation: Campus Handbook Bot
   337	
   338	Out of all four options they listed, this is the smartest pick for your constraints. Here's why:
   339	
   340	Document is clean, typed PDF — no OCR headaches
   341	
   342	Questions are simple and factual — perfect for 1B's capability level
   343	
   344	Relatable demo — anyone can ask "what's the fee refund policy" and immediately judge if it works
   345	
   346	MMMUT has a handbook/prospectus — you already have your dataset
   347	
   348	Citations are natural — "according to section 4.2 of the handbook..."
   349	
   350	Legal simplifier and research paper Q&A both require deeper reasoning that will expose the 1B model's weaknesses. Study notes generator is more generative than retrieval-based. Handbook bot plays to RAG's strengths and hides the SLM's weaknesses.
   351	
   352	Pitfalls to watch — specific to your stack
   353	
   354	Llama 3.2 1B + structured output — if you ask it to return JSON for citations it'll randomly add prose, miss fields, or wrap in markdown. Use a strict system prompt with a hardcoded template and parse defensively. Have a regex fallback.
   355	
   356	Context window is only 128k tokens but effective reasoning degrades fast — keep retrieved chunks to 2-3 max, each under 300 words. Don't stuff the context thinking more = better. With 1B, less context is actually more reliable.
   357	
   358	Ollama on Jetson GPU utilization — Ollama sometimes defaults to CPU on Jetson if the CUDA libraries aren't correctly linked in the container. Verify with ollama run llama3.2:1b and check tegrastats to confirm GPU is actually being used. This is a common silent failure.
   359	
   360	React + FastAPI CORS — trivial issue but will waste an hour if you don't set it up right from day one. Configure CORS middleware in FastAPI before you start frontend work.
   361	
   362	Chunking strategy matters more than people think — naive 500-character chunks will split sentences mid-thought. Use sentence-aware chunking (LlamaIndex's SentenceSplitter) with ~200 token chunks and 20 token overlap. This alone will improve retrieval quality significantly.
   363	
   364	Two person team + vibe coding risk — define who owns what early. Suggested split: one person owns the RAG backend pipeline (ingestion, chunking, embedding, retrieval, Ollama integration), other person owns React frontend + FastAPI routes + Jetson deployment. Don't both touch the same layer.
   365	
   366	Reframed Problem Statement
   367	
   368	"Build an on-device Retrieval-Augmented Generation system on NVIDIA Jetson Orin that serves as an intelligent Campus Handbook Bot. The system ingests institutional PDF documents, builds a persistent semantic vector index, and answers student queries using Llama 3.2 1B via Ollama as the core SLM — retrieving relevant document chunks and generating cited answers entirely on-device. The solution is exposed as a React web interface supporting document upload, natural language Q&A, and citation display, with zero external API calls at any stage."
   369

   371

A student asking "what is rule 4.2.1" will get terrible results from pure semantic search because it's a keyword lookup. Hybrid search handles both cases. LlamaIndex has this built in.
